The 11 subjects match the paper's description of n=11 switch-condition mice.\n\n## 1-c. How are the data split into sessions?\n\ni. Each NWB file corresponds to one session. All `.nwb` files within each subject directory are treated as separate sessions.\n\nii.\n```python\nnwb_files = sorted([f for f in os.listdir(subj_dir) if f.endswith('.nwb')])\n```\n\niii. This yields 152 total sessions (14 per subject, 12 for m11), matching the paper.\n\n## 1-d. How are the data split into trials?\n\ni. Trial boundaries are identified from the `trial_start` and `teleport` behavioral signals. Trial starts are all frames where `trial_start_signal > 0`. Trial ends (teleports) are all frames where `teleport_signal > 0`. The code then pairs them up and filters to ensure each teleport comes after its corresponding trial start.\n\nii.\n```python\ntrial_starts = np.where(trial_start_signal > 0)[0]\nteleports = np.where(teleport_signal > 0)[0]\nn_trials = min(len(trial_starts), len(teleports))\ntrial_starts = trial_starts[:n_trials]\nteleports = teleports[:n_trials]\nvalid = teleports > trial_starts\ntrial_starts = trial_starts[valid]\nteleports = teleports[valid]\n```\n\niii. The AI's CONVERSION_NOTES state that trial boundaries are from `trial_start_inds` to `teleport_inds`. The approach differs from the reference code which detects the rising edge of the teleport signal (transition from <=0 to >0) rather than all positive frames. If the teleport signal is high for multiple consecutive frames, the AI's approach would incorrectly identify multiple \"teleport\" events per trial. However, the AI's resulting trial counts (~80/session) match the paper, suggesting teleport is effectively single-frame in the data.\n\n## 1-e. How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Trials with fewer than 5 timepoints are skipped. Additionally, the AI implements lick error correction: trials where >35% of samples have cumulative lick count > 2 have their lick data set to NaN (but the trials themselves are retained).\n\nii.\n```python\nif n_timepoints < 5:\n    continue  # Skip very short trials\n```\n\nLick error correction:\n```python\nfor t in range(n_trials):\n    start = trial_starts[t]\n    end = teleports[t]\n    trial_lick = lick[start:end]\n    if len(trial_lick) > 0:\n        frac_bad = np.sum(trial_lick > 2) / len(trial_lick)\n        if frac_bad > LICK_ERROR_FRACTION:\n            lick_binary[start:end] = np.nan\n            lick_error_trials.append(t)\n```\n\niii. The AI's CONVERSION_NOTES justify the lick error correction based on the reference code's `get_timeseries_data()` function in `glmUtils.py`, which applies a similar threshold. The reward zone is identified per-trial by computing the mean position where `reward_zone > 0` and matching to the nearest known zone (A, B, or C). For trials where the animal doesn't enter the reward zone, the zone from the previous trial is carried forward.\n\nii. Reward zone identification:\n```python\ndef identify_reward_zone(position, reward_zone_signal, trial_start, trial_end):\n    pos_trial = position[trial_start:trial_end]\n    rz_trial = reward_zone_signal[trial_start:trial_end]\n    in_rz = rz_trial > 0\n    if not np.any(in_rz):\n        return None\n    rz_pos = pos_trial[in_rz]\n    mean_rz_pos = np.mean(rz_pos)\n    best_zone = None\n    best_dist = np.inf\n    for zone_name, (zone_start, zone_end) in REWARD_ZONES.items():\n        zone_center = (zone_start + zone_end) / 2\n        dist = abs(mean_rz_pos - zone_center)\n        if dist < best_dist:\n            best_dist = dist\n            best_zone = zone_name\n    return best_zone\n```\n\nForward-fill for missing zones:\n```python\nlast_known_zone = None\nfor t in range(n_trials):\n    zone = identify_reward_zone(position, reward_zone_signal, trial_starts[t], teleports[t])\n    if zone is not None:\n        last_known_zone = zone\n    trial_rz_label.append(zone if zone is not None else last_known_zone)\n```\n\niii. The AI identifies reward zones from the position where the `reward_zone` signal is positive, mapping to zones A (80-130), B (200-250), C (320-370) based on mean position proximity to zone centers. This differs from the reference's Viterbi algorithm approach but achieves the same goal.\n\n## 7-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Distance to reward zone*?\n\ni. For each timepoint, the signed distance from the animal's position to the nearest edge of the reward zone is computed. Distance is 0 inside the zone, negative before, and positive after.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef compute_distance_to_reward_zone(position, rz_start, rz_end):\n    distance = np.zeros_like(position)\n    before = position < rz_start\n    inside = (position >= rz_start) & (position <= rz_end)\n    after = position > rz_end\n    distance[before] = position[before] - rz_start  # negative\n    distance[inside] = 0.0\n    distance[after] = position[after] - rz_end  # positive\n    return distance\n```\n\niii. This matches the concept of signed distance to the reward zone, consistent with the reference.\n\n## 7-c. How is `output` *Distance to reward zone* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. The continuous distance is discretized into 7 bins using explicit conditional assignments.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef discretize_distance(distance):\n    bins = np.zeros(len(distance), dtype=np.int64)\n    bins[distance < -50] = 0\n    bins[(distance >= -50) & (distance < -10)] = 1\n    bins[(distance >= -10) & (distance < 0)] = 2\n    bins[distance == 0] = 3  # in reward zone\n    bins[(distance > 0) & (distance <= 10)] = 4\n    bins[(distance > 10) & (distance <= 50)] = 5\n    bins[distance > 50] = 6\n    return bins\n```\n\niii. Finding all NWB files:\n```python\ndef get_all_nwb_files(data_dir, sample=False):\n    subjects = sorted([d for d in os.listdir(data_dir)\n                       if d.startswith('sub-') and os.path.isdir(os.path.join(data_dir, d))])\n    all_files = []\n    for subj in subjects:\n        subj_dir = os.path.join(data_dir, subj)\n        nwb_files = sorted([f for f in os.listdir(subj_dir) if f.endswith('.nwb')])\n        for nwb_file in nwb_files:\n            all_files.append({\n                'subject': subj.replace('sub-', ''),\n                'filepath': os.path.join(subj_dir, nwb_file),\n                'filename': nwb_file,\n            })\n    return all_files\n```\n\nLoading data from NWB:\n```python\nwith h5py.File(filepath, 'r') as f:\n    behav = f['processing']['behavior']['BehavioralTimeSeries']\n    position = behav['position']['data'][:]\n    ...\n    ophys = f['processing']['ophys']\n    deconv_data = ophys['Deconvolved']['plane0']['data'][:]\n```\n\niii. The h5py approach reads NWB files at a lower level than pynwb but accesses the same underlying HDF5 data.\n\n## 1-b. How are the data split into subjects?\n\ni. Subjects correspond to subdirectories of `data/` starting with `sub-`. The subject ID is extracted by stripping the `sub-` prefix. Subjects are accumulated into a list as sessions are processed.\n\nii.\n```python\nsubjects = sorted([d for d in os.listdir(data_dir)\n                   if d.startswith('sub-') and os.path.isdir(os.path.join(data_dir, d))])\n...\n'subject': subj.replace('sub-', ''),\n```\n\niii. The 11 subjects match the paper's description of n=11 switch-condition mice.\n\n## 1-c. How are the data split into sessions?\n\ni. Each NWB file corresponds to one session. All `.nwb` files within each subject directory are treated as separate sessions.\n\nii.\n```python\nnwb_files = sorted([f for f in os.listdir(subj_dir) if f.endswith('.nwb')])\n```\n\niii. This yields 152 total sessions (14 per subject, 12 for m11), matching the paper.\n\n## 1-d. How are the data split into trials?\n\ni. Trial boundaries are identified from the `trial_start` and `teleport` behavioral signals. Trial starts are all frames where `trial_start_signal > 0`. Trial ends (teleports) are all frames where `teleport_signal > 0`. The code then pairs them up and filters to ensure each teleport comes after its corresponding trial start.\n\nii.\n```python\ntrial_starts = np.where(trial_start_signal > 0)[0]\nteleports = np.where(teleport_signal > 0)[0]\nn_trials = min(len(trial_starts), len(teleports))\ntrial_starts = trial_starts[:n_trials]\nteleports = teleports[:n_trials]\nvalid = teleports > trial_starts\ntrial_starts = trial_starts[valid]\nteleports = teleports[valid]\n```\n\niii. The AI's CONVERSION_NOTES state that trial boundaries are from `trial_start_inds` to `teleport_inds`. The approach differs from the reference code which detects the rising edge of the teleport signal (transition from <=0 to >0) rather than all positive frames. If the teleport signal is high for multiple consecutive frames, the AI's approach would incorrectly identify multiple \"teleport\" events per trial. However, the AI's resulting trial counts (~80/session) match the paper, suggesting teleport is effectively single-frame in the data.\n\n## 1-e. How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Trials with fewer than 5 timepoints are skipped. Additionally, the AI implements lick error correction: trials where >35% of samples have cumulative lick count > 2 have their lick data set to NaN (but the trials themselves are retained).\n\nii.\n```python\nif n_timepoints < 5:\n    continue  # Skip very short trials\n```\n\nLick error correction:\n```python\nfor t in range(n_trials):\n    start = trial_starts[t]\n    end = teleports[t]\n    trial_lick = lick[start:end]\n    if len(trial_lick) > 0:\n        frac_bad = np.sum(trial_lick > 2) / len(trial_lick)\n        if frac_bad > LICK_ERROR_FRACTION:\n            lick_binary[start:end] = np.nan\n            lick_error_trials.append(t)\n```\n\niii. The AI's CONVERSION_NOTES justify the lick error correction based on the reference code's `get_timeseries_data()` function in `glmUtils.py`, which applies a similar threshold. The 5-timepoint minimum is a lenient filter for very short/degenerate trials.\n\n## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the `neural` data derived from?\n\ni. Neural data is derived from the `Deconvolved` field in the NWB ophys processing module. The AI also loads `Fluorescence` and `Neuropil` data for computing dF/F (used only for interneuron detection, not for the final neural output).\n\nii.\n```python\nophys = f['processing']['ophys']\ndeconv_data = ophys['Deconvolved']['plane0']['data'][:]\nfluor_data = ophys['Fluorescence']['plane0']['data'][:]\nneuropil_data = ophys['Neuropil']['plane0']['data'][:]\n```\n\niii. The AI's CONVERSION_NOTES state: \"NWB files already contain deconvolved activity\" and \"we do NOT need to compute dF/F from scratch.\" The Deconvolved data corresponds to the reference code's `sess.timeseries['events']`.\n\n## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n\ni. The AI applies three processing steps: (1) combines neurons across planes for multi-plane recordings (m17, m18), (2) filters by `iscell` from Suite2p, and (3) additionally filters out putative interneurons identified by high correlation (r > 0.5) between dF/F and speed. NaN values in the neural data are replaced with 0.\n\nii.\n```python\niscell = seg['iscell'][:, 0].astype(bool)\n...\n# Interneuron filtering\nis_interneuron = identify_interneurons(dff, speed, iscell)\nfinal_neuron_mask = np.zeros(n_total_rois, dtype=bool)\nfinal_neuron_mask[iscell_indices[non_interneuron]] = True\nneural_all = deconv_data[:, final_neuron_mask]\n...\n# NaN replacement\ntrial_neural[np.isnan(trial_neural)] = 0\n```\n\niii. The AI's CONVERSION_NOTES cite the paper's description of interneuron exclusion: \"correlation of >0.5\" with speed, excluding \"0.42 +/- 0.85% of cells.\" The AI computed dF/F from Fluorescence/Neuropil data specifically for this filtering step.\n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Two filtering steps: (1) `iscell` from Suite2p manual curation, (2) interneuron exclusion based on Pearson correlation of dF/F with running speed exceeding 0.5.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef identify_interneurons(dff, speed, iscell_mask):\n    ...\n    r = cov_XY / (std_X * std_Y)\n    is_interneuron = r > INTERNEURON_CORR_THRESHOLD  # 0.5\n    return is_interneuron\n```\n\niii. The paper describes both filtering steps. The AI found 0.28% of cells were identified as interneurons (vs 0.42 +/- 0.85% in the paper), which is within the standard deviation range.\n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. Data are aligned to trial start by extracting the neural data segment between `trial_starts[t]` and `teleports[t]` for each trial. Since trials are defined by their start frame, no additional temporal shifting is needed.\n\nii.\n```python\ntrial_neural = neural_all[start:end, :].T.copy()  # (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n```\n\niii. The instructions specify \"Temporally align based on start of the trial,\" which is inherently satisfied by slicing data from trial start to trial end.\n\n## 2-e. What is the temporal resolution (time bin size) of the converted data? Is any temporal rebinning applied?\n\ni. The temporal resolution is one imaging frame (~64.5 ms at 15.5078125 Hz). No rebinning is applied. The AI hardcodes the imaging rate as a constant.\n\nii.\n```python\nIMAGING_RATE = 15.5078125  # Hz\nFRAME_PERIOD = 1.0 / IMAGING_RATE  # seconds\n...\n'time_bin_size': 1000.0 / IMAGING_RATE,  # ms per frame\n```\n\niii. The paper states imaging at ~15.5 Hz. The AI hardcodes this rather than reading it from the data, but the value matches.\n\n## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Time from start of trial in seconds* derived from?\n\ni. This input is derived from the imaging frame rate constant (FRAME_PERIOD = 1/15.5078125 seconds), not from stored timestamps.\n\nii.\n```python\ntime_from_start = np.arange(n_timepoints) * FRAME_PERIOD\n```\n\niii. The AI computes time from the known frame period rather than using actual timestamps stored in the NWB file. Both approaches yield essentially the same values since imaging is at a constant rate.\n\n## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Time from start of trial in seconds*?\n\ni. For each trial, an array of frame indices (0, 1, 2, ...) is multiplied by the frame period to get time in seconds from trial start.\n\nii.\n```python\ntime_from_start = np.arange(n_timepoints) * FRAME_PERIOD\ntrial_input[0, :] = time_from_start\n```\n\niii. This produces monotonically increasing time values starting at 0 for each trial.\n\n## 3-c. How is the `input` *Time from start of trial in seconds* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Both neural data and time-from-start use the same frame indices within each trial (from `trial_starts[t]` to `teleports[t]`), so they are inherently aligned. The time array has the same number of timepoints as the neural data.\n\nii.\n```python\nn_timepoints = end - start\ntime_from_start = np.arange(n_timepoints) * FRAME_PERIOD\ntrial_neural = neural_all[start:end, :].T.copy()\n```\n\niii. Both are sliced from the same frame range, ensuring alignment.\n\n## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Environment type* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from the `environment` behavioral time series in the NWB file.\n\nii.\n```python\nenvironment = behav['environment']['data'][:]\n```\n\niii. The environment variable in the NWB file encodes ENV1=0, ENV2=1.\n\n## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Environment type*?\n\ni. The AI takes the median of valid (non-negative) environment values within each trial, then casts to integer. This is more complex than necessary since environment is constant within a trial.\n\nii.\n```python\ntrial_env = np.zeros(n_trials, dtype=np.int64)\nfor t in range(n_trials):\n    start = trial_starts[t]\n    end = teleports[t]\n    env_vals = environment[start:end]\n    valid_env = env_vals[env_vals >= 0]\n    if len(valid_env) > 0:\n        trial_env[t] = int(np.median(valid_env))\n    else:\n        trial_env[t] = 0\n```\n\niii. Environment is constant within a trial (always 0 or 1), so the median approach yields the same result as directly taking the value.\n\n## 5-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Trial number* derived from?\n\ni. Trial number is the within-session trial index derived from the loop counter over trials (0, 1, 2, ...).\n\nii.\n```python\ntrial_num = float(t)\n...\ntrial_input[2, :] = trial_num\n```\n\niii. The trial number is a sequential index within the session, consistent with the reference approach.\n\n## 5-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Trial number*?\n\ni. No processing beyond assigning the loop index as a float. The value is broadcast to all timepoints within the trial.\n\nii.\n```python\ntrial_input[2, :] = trial_num\n```\n\niii. The trial number is simply the 0-based index of the trial within the session.\n\n## 6-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Previous trial outcome* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from the `Reward` behavioral time series. Reward event timestamps are mapped to frame indices using `np.searchsorted` against behavioral timestamps, then a per-trial `trial_rewarded` boolean array is computed.\n\nii.\n```python\nreward_data = behav['Reward']['data'][:]\nreward_timestamps = behav['Reward']['timestamps'][:]\nbehav_timestamps = behav['position']['timestamps'][:]\nreward_frame_indices = np.searchsorted(behav_timestamps, reward_timestamps)\nreward_frame_indices = np.clip(reward_frame_indices, 0, n_samples - 1)\ntrial_rewarded = np.zeros(n_trials, dtype=bool)\nfor t in range(n_trials):\n    start = trial_starts[t]\n    end = teleports[t]\n    trial_rewards = np.any((reward_frame_indices >= start) & (reward_frame_indices < end))\n    trial_rewarded[t] = trial_rewards\n```\n\niii. The Reward time series has its own timestamps, so `searchsorted` maps them to the nearest behavioral frame.\n\n## 6-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Previous trial outcome*?\n\ni. For each trial t >= 1, the previous trial outcome is set to 1 if `trial_rewarded[t-1]` is True, else 0. For the first trial (t=0), the value defaults to 0.\n\nii.\n```python\nprev_trial_outcome = np.zeros(n_trials, dtype=np.int64)\nfor t in range(1, n_trials):\n    prev_trial_outcome[t] = int(trial_rewarded[t - 1])\n```\n\niii. The instructions specify binary encoding: omitted=0, rewarded=1.\n\n## 7-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Distance to reward zone* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from the `position` behavioral time series and the reward zone location for each trial. The reward zone is identified per-trial by computing the mean position where `reward_zone > 0` and matching to the nearest known zone (A, B, or C). For trials where the animal doesn't enter the reward zone, the zone from the previous trial is carried forward.\n\nii. Reward zone identification:\n```python\ndef identify_reward_zone(position, reward_zone_signal, trial_start, trial_end):\n    pos_trial = position[trial_start:trial_end]\n    rz_trial = reward_zone_signal[trial_start:trial_end]\n    in_rz = rz_trial > 0\n    if not np.any(in_rz):\n        return None\n    rz_pos = pos_trial[in_rz]\n    mean_rz_pos = np.mean(rz_pos)\n    best_zone = None\n    best_dist = np.inf\n    for zone_name, (zone_start, zone_end) in REWARD_ZONES.items():\n        zone_center = (zone_start + zone_end) / 2\n        dist = abs(mean_rz_pos - zone_center)\n        if dist < best_dist:\n            best_dist = dist\n            best_zone = zone_name\n    return best_zone\n```\n\nForward-fill for missing zones:\n```python\nlast_known_zone = None\nfor t in range(n_trials):\n    zone = identify_reward_zone(position, reward_zone_signal, trial_starts[t], teleports[t])\n    if zone is not None:\n        last_known_zone = zone\n    trial_rz_label.append(zone if zone is not None else last_known_zone)\n```\n\niii. The AI identifies reward zones from the position where the `reward_zone` signal is positive, mapping to zones A (80-130), B (200-250), C (320-370) based on mean position proximity to zone centers. This differs from the reference's Viterbi algorithm approach but achieves the same goal.\n\n## 7-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Distance to reward zone*?\n\ni. For each timepoint, the signed distance from the animal's position to the nearest edge of the reward zone is computed. Distance is 0 inside the zone, negative before, and positive after.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef compute_distance_to_reward_zone(position, rz_start, rz_end):\n    distance = np.zeros_like(position)\n    before = position < rz_start\n    inside = (position >= rz_start) & (position <= rz_end)\n    after = position > rz_end\n    distance[before] = position[before] - rz_start  # negative\n    distance[inside] = 0.0\n    distance[after] = position[after] - rz_end  # positive\n    return distance\n```\n\niii. This matches the concept of signed distance to the reward zone, consistent with the reference.\n\n## 7-c. How is `output` *Distance to reward zone* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. The continuous distance is discretized into 7 bins using explicit conditional assignments.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef discretize_distance(distance):\n    bins = np.zeros(len(distance), dtype=np.int64)\n    bins[distance < -50] = 0\n    bins[(distance >= -50) & (distance < -10)] = 1\n    bins[(distance >= -10) & (distance < 0)] = 2\n    bins[distance == 0] = 3  # in reward zone\n    bins[(distance > 0) & (distance <= 10)] = 4\n    bins[(distance > 10) & (distance <= 50)] = 5\n    bins[distance > 50] = 6\n    return bins\n```\n\niii. The bin definitions match the instructions: `< -50`, `-50 to -10`, `-10 to < 0`, `0`, `>0 to +10`, `+10 to +50`, `> +50`.\n\n## 7-d. How is `output` *Distance to reward zone* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Position data and neural data share the same frame indices within each trial, so no additional alignment is needed.\n\nii.\n```python\ntrial_pos = position[start:end]\ndist_to_rz = compute_distance_to_reward_zone(trial_pos, trial_rz_start[t], trial_rz_end[t])\n```\n\niii. Both are sliced from the same `start:end` frame range.\n\n## 8-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Absolute position* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from the `position` behavioral time series in the NWB file.\n\nii.\n```python\nposition = behav['position']['data'][:]\n...\ntrial_pos = position[start:end]\n```\n\niii. The position variable records the animal's location on the VR track in cm.\n\n## 8-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Absolute position*?\n\ni. No processing beyond extracting the per-trial slice and discretizing.\n\nii.\n```python\ntrial_pos = position[start:end]\npos_bins = discretize_position(trial_pos)\n```\n\niii. Raw position values are used directly.\n\n## 8-c. How is `output` *Absolute position* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Position is discretized into 5 bins of 90 cm each (0-90, 90-180, 180-270, 270-360, 360-450) using `np.floor(position / 90)` clipped to [0, 4].\n\nii.\n```python\nPOS_BIN_EDGES = np.linspace(0, TRACK_LENGTH, 6)  # [0, 90, 180, 270, 360, 450]\n\ndef discretize_position(position):\n    bins = np.clip(np.floor(position / 90.0).astype(np.int64), 0, 4)\n    return bins\n```\n\niii. The instructions specify \"5 equal-sized bins.\" The track is 450 cm, so 450/5 = 90 cm per bin. Positions below 0 are clipped to bin 0, above 450 to bin 4. This differs from the reference which uses bins [-inf, 50, 150, 250, 350, inf] (100 cm wide bins with different boundaries).\n\n## 8-d. How is `output` *Absolute position* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Same frame indices as the neural data within each trial.\n\nii.\n```python\ntrial_pos = position[start:end]\n```\n\niii. Same `start:end` slicing ensures alignment.\n\n## 9-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Lick* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from the `lick` behavioral time series in the NWB file.\n\nii.\n```python\nlick = behav['lick']['data'][:]\n```\n\niii. The lick variable records cumulative lick counts per frame.\n\n## 9-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Lick*?\n\ni. Lick data is first binarized by clipping to [0, 1]. Then lick error correction is applied: trials where >35% of samples have cumulative lick count > 2 have their lick data set to NaN. Finally, NaN values are replaced with 0 in the output.\n\nii.\n```python\nlick_binary = np.clip(lick, 0, 1).astype(np.float64)\n\n# Lick error correction per trial\nfor t in range(n_trials):\n    start = trial_starts[t]\n    end = teleports[t]\n    trial_lick = lick[start:end]\n    if len(trial_lick) > 0:\n        frac_bad = np.sum(trial_lick > 2) / len(trial_lick)\n        if frac_bad > LICK_ERROR_FRACTION:\n            lick_binary[start:end] = np.nan\n            lick_error_trials.append(t)\n...\nlick_vals = np.where(np.isnan(trial_lick), 0, trial_lick).astype(np.int64)\n```\n\niii. The AI's CONVERSION_NOTES cite the reference code's lick error correction (fraction threshold 0.35, cumulative lick > 2). The binarization converts the cumulative count to 0/1. However, `np.clip(lick, 0, 1)` clips to [0,1] range rather than thresholding (values between 0 and 1 stay as-is, values >1 become 1). Since lick values are typically integers, this effectively binarizes.\n\n## 9-c. How is `output` *Lick* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Same frame indices as the neural data within each trial.\n\nii.\n```python\ntrial_lick = lick_binary[start:end]\n```\n\niii. Same `start:end` slicing ensures alignment.\n\n## 10-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Reward zone location* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from the `reward_zone` and `position` behavioral time series. The mean position where `reward_zone > 0` is used to identify which of the three zones (A, B, C) is active for each trial.\n\nii. See 7-a for the `identify_reward_zone` function.\n\niii. See 7-a.\n\n## 10-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Reward zone location*?\n\ni. For each trial, the position where `reward_zone > 0` is averaged, and the closest known zone (A=80-130, B=200-250, C=320-370) is assigned based on proximity to zone center. Trials without reward zone entry inherit the previous trial's zone (forward-fill, then backward-fill for leading entries). The zone label is mapped to an integer: A=0, B=1, C=2.\n\nii.\n```python\nrz_label_to_idx = {'A': 0, 'B': 1, 'C': 2}\ntrial_rz_idx = np.array([rz_label_to_idx.get(lbl, 0) for lbl in trial_rz_label])\n...\ntrial_output[4, :] = rz_loc  # broadcast per-trial\n```\n\niii. This differs from the reference's Viterbi algorithm approach, which provides more robust and temporally consistent zone assignments. The AI's forward-fill approach is simpler but could be less robust to noisy position data.\n\n## 11-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Reward outcome* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from the `Reward` behavioral time series (event timestamps).\n\nii.\n```python\nreward_timestamps = behav['Reward']['timestamps'][:]\nreward_frame_indices = np.searchsorted(behav_timestamps, reward_timestamps)\n```\n\niii. The Reward time series has its own timestamps separate from the frame-rate-sampled behavioral data.\n\n## 11-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Reward outcome*?\n\ni. Reward event timestamps are mapped to the nearest behavioral frame index using `searchsorted`. For each trial, the output is 1 if any reward event frame falls within the trial's time range, 0 otherwise. The value is broadcast to all timepoints.\n\nii.\n```python\nreward_frame_indices = np.searchsorted(behav_timestamps, reward_timestamps)\nreward_frame_indices = np.clip(reward_frame_indices, 0, n_samples - 1)\ntrial_rewarded = np.zeros(n_trials, dtype=bool)\nfor t in range(n_trials):\n    start = trial_starts[t]\n    end = teleports[t]\n    trial_rewards = np.any((reward_frame_indices >= start) & (reward_frame_indices < end))\n    trial_rewarded[t] = trial_rewards\n...\ntrial_output[5, :] = reward_out  # broadcast per-trial\n```\n\niii. This approach is consistent with the reference's method of mapping reward timestamps to frame indices and checking for any reward within the trial.\n\n## 12. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n\ni. Several edge cases are handled:\n- **Neural/behavior length mismatch**: If lengths differ, data is truncated to the minimum of the two.\n- **Short trials**: Trials with < 5 timepoints are skipped.\n- **Missing reward zone data**: Trials where `reward_zone` is never active get the previous trial's zone (forward-fill). Leading trials with no zone get the first available zone (backward-fill).\n- **NaN in neural data**: Replaced with 0 (`trial_neural[np.isnan(trial_neural)] = 0`).\n- **Lick error correction**: Trials with >35% of samples having cumulative lick > 2 have lick set to NaN, then NaN replaced with 0 in output.\n- **Sessions with < 2 valid trials**: Skipped entirely.\n- **Multi-plane concatenation**: Handled for animals m17, m18 with 2 imaging planes.\n\nii.\n```python\nn_samples = min(n_behav_samples, n_neural_samples)\nif n_behav_samples != n_neural_samples:\n    position = position[:n_samples]\n    ...\nif n_timepoints < 5:\n    continue\n...\ntrial_neural[np.isnan(trial_neural)] = 0\n```\n\niii. These defensive checks handle known data issues. The neural/behavior mismatch occurs in some sessions.\n\n## 13-a. What are the most time-consuming steps of the code?\n\ni. The AI reports the following timing-consuming steps:\n1. **Loading NWB files** with h5py (I/O bound)\n2. **dF/F computation** for interneuron detection (~4-5 seconds per session with Gaussian filtering and baseline computation)\n3. **Interneuron detection** (vectorized Pearson correlation with speed, optimized from ~4.3s to ~0.4s per session)\n4. **Full conversion**: ~879s (~14.6 min) for 152 sessions\n\nii. The AI includes timing instrumentation:\n```python\nt0 = time.time()\n...\nt_load = time.time() - t0\n...\nt_dff = time.time() - t1\n...\nt_int = time.time() - t1\n```\n\niii. The dF/F computation and interneuron detection are unique to the AI's approach (the reference doesn't do these steps).\n\n## 13-b. What loops in the code could have been vectorized to improve efficiency?\n\ni. The per-trial loop for building input/output arrays could theoretically be vectorized, but variable trial lengths make this awkward. The AI already vectorized the interneuron detection (matrix correlation). The trial reward detection loop iterates over all trials but involves simple indexing operations.\n\nii.\n```python\nfor t in range(n_trials):\n    start = trial_starts[t]\n    end = teleports[t]\n    trial_rewards = np.any((reward_frame_indices >= start) & (reward_frame_indices < end))\n    ...\n```\n\niii. The per-trial structure is natural given variable-length trials. The main loops are already relatively efficient.\n\n## 13-c. What processing does the code repeat multiple times?\n\ni. The AI's code processes each session once (no separate survey step). list of sessions\n        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\n    ] # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\n\n    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\n    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\n    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'metadata': {\n        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\n        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        ...\n    }\n}\n```\n\n### Key Format Requirements\n\n- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n\n- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics such as:\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n\n## Python environment\n\n- All required Python packages are already installed in the system Python environment.\n- Use `python3` to run any python code.\n- If you need to install additional libraries, use `pip install`.\n\n## Decoder Reference\n\n- Run this script with `python train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n- Optional arguments:\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\n\n### Purpose\n\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n\n### Consistency requirements\n\nYour processing and formatting must **match the reference paper and code** with respect to:\n- Loading of data\n- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n\nTo check consistency, you must compare statistics available in the reference paper and your converted dataset. \n\nWhenever possible, invent **SANITY CHECKS** that your loading and processing matches the reference paper and code. \n\nDiscrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above. \n\nCarefully verify your work after every step. frames (~20s at 15.5 Hz)\nDFF_SMOOTH_SIGMA = 2  # frames\n\n# Reward zone definitions (cm)\nREWARD_ZONES = {\n    'A': (80, 130),\n    'B': (200, 250),\n    'C': (320, 370),\n}\n\n# Speed bins for output\nSPEED_BINS = [0, 2, 10, 20, 40, np.inf]  # edges: <2, 2-10, 10-20, 20-40, >40\n\n# Position bins (5 equal bins of 90 cm each)\nPOS_BIN_EDGES = np.linspace(0, TRACK_LENGTH, 6)  # [0, 90, 180, 270, 360, 450]\n\n# Distance to reward zone bins\nDIST_BINS = [-np.inf, -50, -10, 0, 0, 10, 50, np.inf]\n# Special handling: bin 3 = exactly 0 (in reward zone)\n\n\n# ============================================================\n# Helper Functions\n# ============================================================\n\ndef get_all_nwb_files(data_dir, sample=False):\n    \"\"\"Get all NWB files organized by subject.\"\"\"\n    subjects = sorted([d for d in os.listdir(data_dir)\n                       if d.startswith('sub-') and os.path.isdir(os.path.join(data_dir, d))])\n\n    all_files = []\n    for subj in subjects:\n        subj_dir = os.path.join(data_dir, subj)\n        nwb_files = sorted([f for f in os.listdir(subj_dir) if f.endswith('.nwb')])\n        for nwb_file in nwb_files:\n            all_files.append({\n                'subject': subj.replace('sub-', ''),\n                'filepath': os.path.join(subj_dir, nwb_file),\n                'filename': nwb_file,\n            })\n\n    if sample:\n        # Select 2 sessions from different subjects for testing\n        # Pick one small (m11) and one larger subject\n        sample_files = []\n        for f in all_files:\n            if f['subject'] == 'm11' and 'ses-03' in f['filename']:\n                sample_files.append(f)\n            elif f['subject'] == 'm3' and 'ses-03' in f['filename']:\n                sample_files.append(f)\n        return sample_files\n\n    return all_files\n\n\ndef compute_dff(F, Fneu, trial_starts, teleports):\n    \"\"\"\n    Compute dF/F following the reference code preprocessing.dff().\n\n    Steps:\n    1. Neuropil subtraction (coef=0.7)\n    2. Mask out-of-trial data\n    3. Maximin baseline per trial\n    4. dF/F = (F - baseline) / |baseline|\n    5. Gaussian smooth (sigma=2 frames)\n\n    Parameters:\n        F: (n_samples, n_neurons) raw fluorescence\n        Fneu: (n_samples, n_neurons) neuropil fluorescence\n        trial_starts: array of trial start indices\n        teleports: array of trial end indices\n\n    Returns:\n        dff: (n_samples, n_neurons) delta F/F, NaN outside trials\n    \"\"\"\n    n_samples, n_neurons = F.shape\n\n    # Neuropil subtraction\n    F_corr = F - NEUROPIL_COEF * Fneu\n\n    # Initialize dFF with NaN\n    dff = np.full_like(F_corr, np.nan)\n\n    for i, (start, stop) in enumerate(zip(trial_starts, teleports)):\n        if start >= n_samples or stop >= n_samples:\n            continue\n        trial_F = F_corr[start:stop, :].copy()\n\n        if len(trial_F) < 10:\n            continue\n\n        # Maximin baseline per trial\n        smoothed = gaussian_filter1d(trial_F, sigma=15, axis=0)\n        min_filtered = minimum_filter1d(smoothed, size=BASELINE_WINDOW, axis=0)\n        baseline = maximum_filter1d(min_filtered, size=BASELINE_WINDOW, axis=0)\n\n        # dF/F\n        abs_baseline = np.abs(baseline)\n        abs_baseline[abs_baseline < 1e-10] = 1e-10\n        trial_dff = (trial_F - baseline) / abs_baseline\n\n        # Gaussian smooth within trial (combined loop)\n        dff[start:stop, :] = gaussian_filter1d(trial_dff, sigma=DFF_SMOOTH_SIGMA, axis=0)\n\n    return dff\n\n\ndef identify_interneurons(dff, speed, iscell_mask):\n    \"\"\"\n    Identify putative interneurons by correlation of dF/F with speed.\n    Vectorized implementation for performance.\n\n    Parameters:\n        dff: (n_samples, n_neurons) delta F/F\n        speed: (n_samples,) speed timeseries\n        iscell_mask: boolean mask for which ROIs are cells\n\n    Returns:\n        is_interneuron: boolean array of length n_iscell (True = interneuron)\n    \"\"\"\n    n_neurons = iscell_mask.sum()\n    dff_cells = dff[:, iscell_mask]  # (n_samples, n_neurons)\n\n    # Find globally valid timepoints (speed is valid)\n    speed_valid = ~np.isnan(speed)\n\n    # For each neuron, valid = non-NaN dff AND non-NaN speed\n    # Since dFF NaN pattern is the same for all neurons (based on trial boundaries),\n    # we can use the first neuron's pattern\n    neuron_valid = ~np.isnan(dff_cells[:, 0]) if n_neurons > 0 else np.zeros(len(speed), dtype=bool)\n    valid = speed_valid & neuron_valid\n\n    if valid.sum() < 100:\n        return np.zeros(n_neurons, dtype=bool)\n\n    # Vectorized Pearson correlation\n    X = dff_cells[valid, :]  # (n_valid, n_neurons)\n    Y = speed[valid]  # (n_valid,)\n\n    # Demean\n    X_mean = X.mean(axis=0, keepdims=True)\n    Y_mean = Y.mean()\n    X_centered = X - X_mean\n    Y_centered = Y - Y_mean\n\n    # Correlation = cov(X, Y) / (std(X) * std(Y))\n    n = valid.sum()\n    cov_XY = (X_centered * Y_centered[:, np.newaxis]).sum(axis=0) / (n - 1)\n    std_X = np.sqrt((X_centered ** 2).sum(axis=0) / (n - 1))\n    std_Y = np.sqrt((Y_centered ** 2).sum() / (n - 1))\n\n    # Avoid division by zero\n    std_X[std_X < 1e-10] = 1e-10\n\n    r = cov_XY / (std_X * std_Y)\n\n    is_interneuron = r > INTERNEURON_CORR_THRESHOLD\n    return is_interneuron\n\n\ndef identify_reward_zone(position, reward_zone_signal, trial_start, trial_end):\n    \"\"\"\n    Identify which reward zone (A, B, or C) is active for a trial.\n\n    Uses position where reward_zone > 0 to determine zone location.\n    \"\"\"\n    pos_trial = position[trial_start:trial_end]\n    rz_trial = reward_zone_signal[trial_start:trial_end]\n\n    in_rz = rz_trial > 0\n    if not np.any(in_rz):\n        return None  # No reward zone entry (omission or no entry)\n\n    rz_pos = pos_trial[in_rz]\n    mean_rz_pos = np.mean(rz_pos)\n\n    # Map to zone A, B, or C based on center position\n    best_zone = None\n    best_dist = np.inf\n    for zone_name, (zone_start, zone_end) in REWARD_ZONES.items():\n        zone_center = (zone_start + zone_end) / 2\n        dist = abs(mean_rz_pos - zone_center)\n        if dist < best_dist:\n            best_dist = dist\n            best_zone = zone_name\n\n    return best_zone\n\n\ndef compute_distance_to_reward_zone(position, rz_start, rz_end):\n    \"\"\"\n    Compute signed distance from position to nearest point in reward zone.\n\n    Negative = before zone (position < rz_start)\n    Zero = within zone\n    Positive = past zone (position > rz_end)\n    \"\"\"\n    distance = np.zeros_like(position)\n\n    before = position < rz_start\n    inside = (position >= rz_start) & (position <= rz_end)\n    after = position > rz_end\n\n    distance[before] = position[before] - rz_start  # negative\n    distance[inside] = 0.0\n    distance[after] = position[after] - rz_end  # positive\n\n    return distance\n\n\ndef discretize_distance(distance):\n    \"\"\"\n    Discretize distance to reward zone into 7 bins:\n    0: < -50 cm\n    1: -50 to -10 cm\n    2: -10 cm to < 0 cm\n    3: 0 cm (in reward zone)\n    4: >0 cm to +10 cm\n    5: +10 to +50 cm\n    6: > +50 cm\n    \"\"\"\n    bins = np.zeros(len(distance), dtype=np.int64)\n\n    bins[distance < -50] = 0\n    bins[(distance >= -50) & (distance < -10)] = 1\n    bins[(distance >= -10) & (distance < 0)] = 2\n    bins[distance == 0] = 3  # in reward zone\n    bins[(distance > 0) & (distance <= 10)] = 4\n    bins[(distance > 10) & (distance <= 50)] = 5\n","exit_code":0,"status":"completed"}}

range\n        # distance is 0 if inside [lo, hi], otherwise distance to nearest edge\n        # for NaN trials, uniform emission (all zeros) so assignment is driven by transitions\n Use (4,) for per-trial inputs and time_from_start as time-varying\n        # Actually, the spec says input can be (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n        # Let's make time_from_start time-varying and others per-trial\n        # We'll pack as: row 0 = time_from_start (time-varying)\n        # rows 1-3 = per-trial values broadcast to time-varying\n        trial_input = np.zeros((4, n_timepoints), dtype=np.float32)\n        trial_input[0, :] = time_from_start\n        trial_input[1, :] = env_val\n        trial_input[2, :] = trial_num\n        trial_input[3, :] = prev_outcome\n\n        # Output construction\n        trial_pos = position[start:end]\n        trial_speed = speed[start:end]\n        trial_lick = lick_binary[start:end]\n\n        # [0] Distance to reward zone (time-varying, discretized)\n        dist_to_rz = compute_distance_to_reward_zone(trial_pos, trial_rz_start[t], trial_rz_end[t])\n        dist_bins = discretize_distance(dist_to_rz)\n\n        # [1] Absolute position (time-varying, discretized)\n        pos_bins = discretize_position(trial_pos)\n\n        # [2] Speed (time-varying, discretized)\n        speed_bins = discretize_speed(trial_speed)\n\n        # [3] Lick (time-varying, binary)\n        lick_vals = np.where(np.isnan(trial_lick), 0, trial_lick).astype(np.int64)\n\n        # [4] Reward zone location (per trial)\n        rz_loc = trial_rz_idx[t]\n\n        # [5] Reward outcome (per trial)\n        reward_out = int(trial_rewarded[t])\n\n        # Output: time-varying outputs as (n_output, n_timepoints), per-trial as (n_output,)\n        # Mix: first 4 are time-varying, last 2 are per-trial\n        # Pack all as (6, n_timepoints) with per-trial broadcast\n        trial_output = np.zeros((6, n_timepoints), dtype=np.int64)\n        trial_output[0, :] = dist_bins\n        trial_output[1, :] = pos_bins\n        trial_output[2, :] = speed_bins\n        trial_output[3, :] = lick_vals\n        trial_output[4, :] = rz_loc  # broadcast per-trial\n        trial_output[5, :] = reward_out  # broadcast per-trial\n\n        neural_trials.append(trial_neural.astype(np.float32))\n        input_trials.append(trial_input)\n        output_trials.append(trial_output)\n        valid_trials.append(t)\n\n    t_total = time.time() - t0\n\n    session_info = {\n        'subject': subject_id,\n        'session': session_id,\n        'n_neurons': n_neurons,\n        'n_interneurons': n_interneurons,\n        'n_iscell': int(iscell.sum()),\n        'n_trials': len(valid_trials),\n        'n_trials_total': n_trials,\n        'n_lick_error_trials': len(lick_error_trials),\n        'n_rewarded': int(trial_rewarded.sum()),\n        'imaging_rate': imaging_rate,\n        'n_planes': n_planes,\n        'load_time': t_load,\n        'dff_time': t_dff,\n        'interneuron_time': t_int,\n        'total_time': t_total,\n        'reward_zones': trial_rz_label,\n        'environments': trial_env.tolist(),\n    }\n\n    print(f\"  {subject_id} ses-{session_id}: {n_neurons} neurons ({n_interneurons} interneurons excluded), \"\n          f\"{len(valid_trials)} trials, {int(trial_rewarded.sum())}/{n_trials} rewarded, \"\n          f\"{len(lick_error_trials)} lick-error trials, \"\n          f\"{t_total:.1f}s\")\n\n    # ---- Optional processing visualization ----\n    if show_processing and session_idx < 2:\n        plot_processing(filepath, subject_id, session_id,\n                        position, speed, lick_binary, trial_starts, teleports,\n                        neural_all, trial_rz_start, trial_rz_end,\n                        trial_rewarded, trial_env, valid_trials,\n                        neural_trials, input_trials, output_trials,\n                        session_idx)\n\n    return neural_trials, input_trials, output_trials, session_info\n\n\ndef plot_processing(filepath, subject_id, session_id,\n                    position, speed, lick, trial_starts, teleports,\n                    neural_all, trial_rz_start, trial_rz_end,\n                    trial_rewarded, trial_env, valid_trials,\n                    neural_trials, input_trials, output_trials,\n                    session_idx):\n    \"\"\"Plot processing steps for visual verification.\"\"\"\n    import matplotlib\n    matplotlib.use('Agg')\n    import matplotlib.pyplot as plt\n\n    fig, axes = plt.subplots(6, 2, figsize=(20, 24))\n    fig.suptitle(f'Processing: {subject_id} ses-{session_id}', fontsize=16)\n\n    # Pick a representative trial\n    trial_idx = min(5, len(valid_trials) - 1)\n    t = valid_trials[trial_idx]\n    start = trial_starts[t]\n    end = teleports[t]\n    n_tp = end - start\n    time_sec = np.arange(n_tp) * FRAME_PERIOD\n\n    # Panel 1: Raw position and speed for one trial\n    ax = axes[0, 0]\n    ax.plot(time_sec, position[start:end], label='Position (cm)')\n    ax.axhline(y=trial_rz_start[t], color='r', linestyle='--', label=f'RZ start ({trial_rz_start[t]:.0f} cm)')\n    ax.axhline(y=trial_rz_end[t], color='r', linestyle=':', label=f'RZ end ({trial_rz_end[t]:.0f} cm)')\n    ax.set_ylabel('Position (cm)')\n    ax.set_xlabel('Time (s)')\n    ax.legend(fontsize=8)\n    ax.set_title(f'Trial {t}: Position')\n\n    ax = axes[0, 1]\n    ax.plot(time_sec, speed[start:end], label='Speed (cm/s)')\n    ax.axhline(y=SPEED_THRESHOLD, color='r', linestyle='--', label=f'Threshold ({SPEED_THRESHOLD} cm/s)')\n    ax.set_ylabel('Speed (cm/s)')\n    ax.set_xlabel('Time (s)')\n    ax.legend(fontsize=8)\n    ax.set_title(f'Trial {t}: Speed')\n\n    # {"type":"item.completed","item":{"id":"item_15","type":"command_execution","command":"/bin/bash -lc \"sed -n '261,520p' /tests/reference_DECISIONS.md\"","aggregated_output":"\nii.\n```python\ninput_curr[2] = trial\n```\n\niii. The trial number is simply the sequential index of the trial within the session.\n\n## 6-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Previous trial outcome* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from the `Reward` behavior time series. Reward event timestamps are matched to behavior timestamps, and a binary `isreward` array marks timepoints where rewards occurred.\n\nii.\n```python\nreward_amounts = behavior_data['Reward'].data[:][:T]\nreward_times = behavior_data['Reward'].timestamps[:]\nreward_time_indices = np.searchsorted(timestamps, reward_times)\nisreward = np.zeros(T)\nisreward[reward_time_indices] = 1\n```\n\niii. The `Reward` time series has its own timestamps (not aligned to the behavior sampling rate), so `searchsorted` is used to find the nearest timepoint. An assertion checks that the alignment error is within half a time bin.\n\n## 6-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Previous trial outcome*?\n\ni. For each trial, check whether any reward event occurred in the *previous* trial's time range. For the first trial, set to 0. The value is constant across all timepoints within a trial.\n\nii.\n```python\nif trial == 0:\n    input_curr[3,:] = 0\nelse:\n    prev_reward_idx = np.arange(trial_start_idx[trial-1], trial_end_idx[trial-1], dtype=int)\n    isprevreward = np.any(isreward[prev_reward_idx])\n    input_curr[3,:] = int(isprevreward)\n```\n\niii. The instructions specify previous trial outcome as binary (omitted=0, rewarded=1). The code checks if any reward event fell within the previous trial's time boundaries.\n\n## 7-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Distance to reward zone* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from the `position` behavior time series and the reward zone location for the current trial. The reward zone boundaries come from the `reward_zone` behavior time series. This variable is non-zero when the mouse is in the reward zone. I combine this with the position of the mouse when this is non-zero to figure out the closest reward zone. To allow for some noise (of unknown source) and unrewarded tasks, I use the Viterbi algorithm to assign zones so that the rewarded positions match the known reward zones and it is uncommon to switch reward zone. \n\nii. Assigning reward zone location for each trial: \n- Before converting, the `survey()` function was used to try to understand all the data values, including whatever is going on with the `reward_zone` variable. It first computes the position of the min and max position of the mouse when `reward_zone` is non-zero:\n```python\nbehavior_data = nwbfile.processing['behavior']['BehavioralTimeSeries'].time_series       \ninfo['reward_zone_positions'] = np.full((ntrials, 2), np.nan)\nposition = behavior_data['position'].data[:]\nfor trial in range(ntrials):\n    idx = np.arange(trial_start_idx[trial], trial_end_idx[trial], dtype=int)\n    pos_trial = position[idx]\n    reward_zone_trial = reward_zone[idx] > 0\n    if not np.any(reward_zone_trial):\n        continue\n    info['reward_zone_positions'][trial] = [np.min(pos_trial[reward_zone_trial]), np.max(pos_trial[reward_zone_trial])]\n```\nThe function `segment_reward_positions()` uses the Viterbi algorithm to assign reward zone labels so that the initial rewarded positions are close to the corresponding rewarded zone:\n```python\nreward_zone_dict = {'A': [80, 130],\n                    'B': [200, 250],\n                    'C': [320, 370]}\n...\nsigma = 10.0  # std dev for Gaussian emission on zone distance\nzone_ranges = np.array([reward_zone_dict[z] for z in zone_names])  # (n_states, 2)\ndist = np.maximum(zone_ranges[None, :, 0] - startpoints[valid, None],\n                    startpoints[valid, None] - zone_ranges[None, :, 1]) # negative if inside the zone, positive if outside\ndist = np.maximum(dist, 0)\nlog_emission[valid] = -0.5 * (dist / sigma) ** 2\n```\nIt also encourages the reward zone label to not change often:\n```python\npstay = .9\nlog_trans = np.full((n_states, n_states), np.log((1 - pstay) / (n_states - 1)))\nnp.fill_diagonal(log_trans, np.log(pstay))\n```\n\niii. The paper defines reward zones A, B, C at specific position ranges. These same ranges could also be found, with some translation, in the code (required mapping X -> A, etc.). I plotted the position of the mouse when `reward_zone` was positive (note that the value was between 0 and 6 I think) to determine that the Viterbi approach would likely work, and that the start of the rewarded position was the least noisy variable to use. The class fractions were approximately 1/3 after this, so it seemed correct. \n\n## 7-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Distance to reward zone*?\n\ni. For each timepoint, compute the signed distance from the animal's position to the nearest edge of the reward zone. Distance is 0 when inside the zone, negative when before the zone, and positive when past it.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef compute_distance_to_reward_zone(position_curr, reward_zone_curr):\n    ntimepoints = len(position_curr)\n    reward_zone_range = reward_zone_dict[reward_zone_curr]\n    distance_to_reward_zone = np.zeros(ntimepoints)\n    idx_before_zone = position_curr < reward_zone_range[0]\n    distance_to_reward_zone[idx_before_zone] = position_curr[idx_before_zone] - reward_zone_range[0]\n    idx_after_zone = position_curr > reward_zone_range[1]\n    distance_to_reward_zone[idx_after_zone] = position_curr[idx_after_zone] - reward_zone_range[1]\n    return distance_to_reward_zone\n```\n\niii. This matches the paper's concept of distance relative to the reward zone. The reward zone boundaries are from `reward_zone_dict`, inspired by the reference code `Sosa_et_al_2024/src/reward_relative/behavior.py`.\n\n## 7-c. How is `output` *Distance to reward zone* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. The continuous distance is discretized into 7 bins using `np.digitize` with bin edges `[-inf, -50, -10, 0, 1e-6, 10, 50, inf]`.\n\nii.\n```python\ndistance_to_reward_zone_bins = [-np.inf, -50, -10, 0, 1e-6, 10, 50, np.inf]\n...\noutput_curr[0] = np.digitize(distance_to_reward_zone, distance_to_reward_zone_bins) - 1\n```\n\niii. The bin edges match the instructions. The `1e-6` boundary separates exactly 0 (in the zone boundary) from slightly positive. The `-1` corrects `np.digitize` to be 0-indexed.\n\n## 7-d. How is `output` *Distance to reward zone* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. The position data and neural data share the same time indices within each trial, so no additional alignment is needed.\n\nii.\n```python\nidx = np.arange(trial_start_idx[trial], trial_end_idx[trial], dtype=int)\nspike_data_curr = spike_data[idx]\nposition_curr = position[idx]\n```\n\niii. X_mean\n   175\t    Y_centered = Y - Y_mean\n   176\t\n   177\t    # Correlation = cov(X, Y) / (std(X) * std(Y))\n   178\t    n = valid.sum()\n   179\t    cov_XY = (X_centered * Y_centered[:, np.newaxis]).sum(axis=0) / (n - 1)\n   180\t    std_X = np.sqrt((X_centered ** 2).sum(axis=0) / (n - 1))\n   181\t    std_Y = np.sqrt((Y_centered ** 2).sum() / (n - 1))\n   182\t\n   183\t    # Avoid division by zero\n   184\t    std_X[std_X < 1e-10] = 1e-10\n   185\t\n   186\t    r = cov_XY / (std_X * std_Y)\n   187\t\n   188\t    is_interneuron = r > INTERNEURON_CORR_THRESHOLD\n   189\t    return is_interneuron\n   190\t\n   191\t\n   192\tdef identify_reward_zone(position, reward_zone_signal, trial_start, trial_end):\n   193\t    \"\"\"\n   194\t    Identify which reward zone (A, B, or C) is active for a trial.\n   195\t\n   196\t    Uses position where reward_zone > 0 to determine zone location.\n   197\t    \"\"\"\n   198\t    pos_trial = position[trial_start:trial_end]\n   199\t    rz_trial = reward_zone_signal[trial_start:trial_end]\n   200\t\n   201\t    in_rz = rz_trial > 0\n   202\t    if not np.any(in_rz):\n   203\t        return None  # No reward zone entry (omission or no entry)\n   204\t\n   205\t    rz_pos = pos_trial[in_rz]\n   206\t    mean_rz_pos = np.mean(rz_pos)\n   207\t\n   208\t    # Map to zone A, B, or C based on center position\n   209\t    best_zone = None\n   210\t    best_dist = np.inf\n   211\t    for zone_name, (zone_start, zone_end) in REWARD_ZONES.items():\n   212\t        zone_center = (zone_start + zone_end) / 2\n   213\t        dist = abs(mean_rz_pos -
